INTRODUCTION.


This volume commences with the S#af-chakra-niripana,
of Purzananda Svāmī which forms the sixth chapter of the
hitherto unpublished work of that author entitled Shritattva-
chintamazi. The latter is a voluminous treatise in Twenty-five
Chapters. The MSS. in the library of the Asiatic Society
of Bengal covers some 400 pages of close writing on paper
larger than foolscap.

The first Chapter deals with Tattvabodha ; the Second with
the necessity of and different modes of Diksha ; the Third
Chapter gives directions regarding the selection of the place of
Diksha, Kirmmachakra, Vastuyaga and Vighnanashaka
Mazdala (danger-destroying circle) ; also as to the appropriate
sacrifices and Mantras of the Devatas to be worshipped in
the Mandala ; the Fourth Chapter is devoted to the rules
relating to the erection of the Mamdapa; the Fifth Chapter
speaks of the auxiliary Devatas and their places in Diksha 9
and the rulas relating to their Раја ; of the kumbha (jar); of
the metals to be used in its making and its size and shape.

The sixth chapter of it here published contains a description
of the six centres, the socalled Mialadhara Svadishthaina,
Mazipura, Anahata, Vishuddha, and Ajna Chakras ог Lotuses
(padma) a knowledge of which is essential for the understanding
of the Yoga method dealt with in the Tantras. To this work
there is a commentary, apparently written by Kali-charana.
Though there is no direct statement of such authorship yet the
commentaries on the S#a¢-chakra and the Paduka-panchaka are
written in the same style, and under v. 43 of the лаг chakra
the Commentator says that he has made clear the matter
there referred to in the commentary ( which was written by
Kalicharama) on the Paduka-panchaka-stotra (paduka panchaka
stotre spashti-kritam). Kalicharaza begins his commentary on
the Padukapanchaka by saying that it is composed by himself


=== | Kalicharanak prakashayet ). To the commentary of Kali-
charana are added notes by the Commentator Shankara who
appears to have used a text slightly different from that of
Kalicharana. The variant readings have been here noted.

This work and its commentaries are followed by the
Paduka-panchaka with the commentary of Kalicharana already
referred to. The latter is a careful commentator, who has dealt
with his subject in a thorough manner but the difficulties of
both the text and his commentary are such that the reader
will be glad to avail himself of the notes from the third
commentary here referred to, by Vishvanatha on the second
Pavala of the Kaivalya-kalika Tantra which, ( though so far as
I am aware not now available ) dealt, in this portion of it, with
the same subject and much on the same lines as Purnananda’s
Shat-chakra. Rather, it should be said that, as the Kaivalya-
kalika is held to have been an original Tantra, it is not unlikely
that Pirnananda was indebted to it for his own work, In
some matters, Vishvanatha has explained things in his own
way, simply and in a manner which goes straight to the point.

The Shat-chakra contains fifty five verses: To these is
added another, here numbered 37 (т) and located at the end
of the volume which though not noticed by either Kali-charaza
or Shangkara, is given by Baladeva’s text.

The work may be conveniently divided into eight sections,
namely, (т) verses І to 3 which deal with the Nadis, (2) verses
4 to тз treating of the Muladhara Padma, (3) verses 40 8,
ता he 5050550252), (4) verses тд to 2: with
Малірӣга, (5) verses 22 to 27 with Anahata, (6) verses Zk
зі with Vishuddha, (7) verses 32 to 38 with Ajna, (8) verses 39
to 49 with the Sahasrara, and (9) verses 50 to 54 with the
passage of Kundalini from the Mūlādhāra to Sahasrara piercing
on her way thither the six chakras—a Tantrika Yoga Process
known as Shad-chakra-bheda or as some call it Bhatashuddhi.

It is not necessary to deal further with the subject matter
here, as I have, in preparation for publication, an English trans.
lation of the text now given. The Shat-chakra-nirupava ang


the Paduka-panchaka ] notes of Kalicharaza were first
published in Shakābda, ı778 (А. D. ї858 ) by the Sucharu
Press, Calcutta, under the editorship of the late Anandachandra
Vedantavagisha, This edition which is rare, having been long
out of print, has been used in the preparation of this volume.
In I276 (A.D. 869) another edition of the S#a¢-chakra with
the commentary of Ramavallabha was published by Kalidas
Bhattacharyya, along with the Paduka-panchaka and its com-
mentary by Kali-charaza. Kaliprasanna Vidyaratna in ї204
(A.D. I887) published the Séa¢-chakra together with a number
of other books on Yoga. This edition of the Sfa¢-chakra
contained the commentary of Rama-vallabha though it is not
acknowledged as such by the editor. There have been several
other cheap publications’ on Yoga which contain the .5/аг-
chakra-niripaza. Some are provided with Bengali translations
but none with notes. The commentaries of Vishvanatha and
the notes from Shangkara are published for the first time.


CALCUTTA. ARTHUR AVALON.
January 2973.

By


Shrimat Svami Bhairabananda


The two original texts published in this volume are of great
importance to all classes of Indian Sadhakas.

(i) Padukipanchakam.—A clear description of the
five-fold foot-stool or seat of the Guru (the Supreme Self )
uttered from the mouth of Shiva himself who is the revealer
of all the Tantras ( True knowledge ). This description agrees
generally with the account given by Purnananda in the
Shatchakranirupanam incorporated in this volume.

(2) Shatchakranirupanam Љу Paramahamsa Svami
Purnananda—a very celebrated Tintrika Sadhaka of the
їбїһ century A. D. He was born of a very respectable
is family of Bengal in the district of Mymensingh,
It is said that he could not accept and follow’ in detail the
nature of the Chakras or the subtle Centres in the human
body and the exhaustive description given to him by his
Guru (spiritual guide ) who was a great seer. Subsequently
when his Guru was pleased to grant him the eye of wisdom,
the true nature of the Centres which are very subtle was
revealed to Purnananda. He afterwards recorded a vivid
of what he actually visualised for the benefit
of future Sadhakas, interested in the Kundalini Voga,
in the Shatchakranirupanam. This work forms a part of hig
great work named the Shritattvachintamant which is a very
authoritative book on Agama Shastra. He also wrote
many other books on Tantrik philosophy and rituals ete,
which are held in high esteem by all classes of worshippers
in India.

The form of Yoga dealt with in the Shatchakran
published in this volume is variously known amon
Indian Sadhakas as Kundalini Yoga’ ‘Laya Yoga’ or
Chakra-Bheda’, /


account


irupanam
gst t he


‘Shat.


=| OREWORD 5


Very powerful Yogis only can achieve this highest form
of Tantrika Yoga by which the supreme inherent power in
the human body which normally remains (dormant) coiled like a
serpent at the base of the spine, and which is called, for this
reason, by the name of Kundalinz shakti is uncoiled and
made to pass through the six Principal ‘Chakras’ or Centres
of the subtle body which are in the form of lotuses.

This process generates а wonderful power and a new
vitality in the whole psycho-physical organism. As it also
purifies the elements of the body, it is sometimes called by
the name of '‘Bhuta-Shuddh?’.

Though this form of Yoga is mentioned in Yoga Upanishads
and Puranas, this has attained the fullest development
and perfection іп the Tantras and Agamas. According to the
Tantras the chief centres of consciousness are the Chakras
existing within the Cerebro-Spinal Axis, the highest centre,
the Sakasr@ra or the ‘thousand-petalled Lotus’ being located
within the Cerebrum or the upper brain.

As the ‘Chakras’? or Centres are very subtle, their
‘Svarupa’ ог true nature can not be described from the
anatomical or the physiological stand-point. An attempt to
trace them in the gross body, has been a very common blunder
of a multitude of modern enquirers. Though the Chakras
may ultimately have relations with gross physical functions
they do not exist as parts of the gross body, but are subtle
Centres on which concentration is practised or done.

The authorities on this particular system of Yoga consider
it inadvisable to be practised except by the fully competent
(«४८४८६७८ ). In order to qualify oneself for initiation into
Yoga one must be intelligent, with senses controlled, abstaining
from injury to all beings, ever doing good to all, a believer in
Vedas and Guru, whose faith and ultimate refuge is in
Brakman and who is a non-dualist. It will appear from the
above that this competency is to be achieved after following
reverentially a course of instructions laid down in the
Shasivas and under proper guidance.


7 5 OREWORD


The above competency is to be determined by his
Guru (spiritual guide ) who alone can unfold the true nature
of the Yoga and who alone is competent to give safe
and reliable practical instructions as to the effective methods
to be followed. His guidance and help are indispensable for
a Sadhaka to tide over certain incidental difficulties and
dangers, which are very common to a beginner.

“Aikyam jiwatmanorahur yogam yogavishavadah”’,

The term Yoga means the process by which /ivatman or
embodied Self or Consciousness achieves union with
Paramatman or the Supreme Soul in its true form of Being,
Consciousness and Bliss, апа its correct practice is sure to
lead to the achievement of their identity.

The Vedas proclaim that “all this is verily the Brakman»
( Sarvam khalvidam буайта у and that the Paramatman ig
amrita ( imperishable ) ananta ( endless ) and Gnandasvarupe
(all blissful ), How is it then, it may be asked, that the
individual self the /zv@/man who is a part and parcel of the
Paramatman the Supreme soul, is so full of ignorance, limita.
tions and miseries? The answer is this. By the ivarang
(veiling ) and vikskepa ( projecting ) shaktis ( powers ) of the
Maya, resident in the Brakman, the dualistic world appears to
be brought into being whereby the true nature of the self į
veiled ( Aanchukita ) and a sense of apurnata ( imperfectness
is produced. The consciousness veils itself to ‘itself, ang
projects or throws forward from within itself, a phenomena]
world of finite things and minds which makes it look likę
limited and thwarted. In this way the Purna seems to bg
apurna, the infinite seems to be the finite, and it 3 thg
inscrutable powers of the Aaya that have done the trick.

The Kundalini Yoga enables the individual to 566 throug),
the veil of Maya which obscures the true nature of the self,
One of the main principles underlying this Yoga is that the
apparent imperfection of the /zva@iman can only be ०५६५०७
through the realisation of the identity of itself with the
Pavamaiman.


= OREWORD 7


The sense of imperfectness and the natural inclination
and yearning for happiness ( Axandam ) are a sure indication
that the /zatman entangled in the process of Creation, feels
within itself a sense of temporary incompleteness which moves
it on to strive for the realisation of its true and eternal nature
as Being, Consciousness and Bliss ( Sat-chidanandasvariipa )
which is usually obscured by the Avidyg or ignorance caused
by the veil of Maya.

All forms of religious practice and Yoga are prescribed
to free the individual from the bonds of illusion and to see,
through the eye of wisdom granted by the All-Merciful Guru,
whose abode is the Sahasrira or the thousand-petalled Lotus.
The Kundalint who sleeps coiled in the Maladhara. closing
with her mouth the entrance to the Sushumna Nadi called
the Bramhadvara, is the Sabdabrahman. All Mantras are
but her manifestations and the letters are the Matrika@s or the
genetrices of all the Universes in the World-Consciousness.
It is she who creates and binds Jwa in body and again it
is she who withdraws the bonds herself, by revealing the
Tattvajnana and the process by which Liberation may be
attained. Mantras are nothing but manifestations of Kula-
Rundatint herself and as such are effective instruments to
rouse her from her static state.

The first step in the acquirement of true knowledge is an
acquaintance with and practice of the Tantrika Yoga Sadhana,
which deals with the Chakras, Nad;s, Kundalini, Asana,
Prinayama, Vedhas, Bandhas, Mudra@s and other processes.
Next is the realisation of that process by rousing the Kundalini
which finally leads to the direct realisation of the Supreme
Brahman—the supreme experience of the union of the Jzvatman
with the Paramitman, the individual with the Supreme Self.

The power which is aroused is in itself pure Consciousness
and when aroused and taken to the uppermost Cerebral Centre,
is the dispeller of all Pashudhavas (animal propensities in man)
and revealer of the Divine state due to the true knowledge
of the Self which is the Supreme Consciousness or Para


2


8 FOREWORD


Samvit. At this stage the Kundalini unites in simarasya
with the Pavamashiva and assumes the state of Mahakundalinz.

This Yoga has been rightly regarded as the greatest of all
forms of Yoga practices ( Sarva-yogo-tlamottamah ). It
incorporates all that is good in other systems of Yoga dis-
cipline. While recognising that man is in essence spirit, it does
not shut its eyes to the patent fact that the human individua]
is a psycho-physical organism, Any system, which ignores
this is bound to be lop-sided, It is really doubtful whether
spiritual advancement of the highest type is at all possible
without a parallel progress and development in the physica]
and the intellectual planes, It is a peculiar merit of the
Kundalint Yoga that in its comprehensive programme,
physical, mental and spiritual powers and faculties arg
harmoniously controlied and developed in equal pace for the
achievement of the Supreme End. When this Yoga is practiseg
in the right spirit, the Sadhaka gradually realises that his life
{ all his activities, physical or mental, vital or spiritua],
are but a part of the Divine activities of the Universal Mother,
manifesting and operating through a particular individua]
human centre. The body is not something base or undivine,
The world or its enjoyments are not things (0 be despised Or
shunned, for all things are but emanations of the Universa]
Consciousness ( Chit- Shakti у, manifestations of the One ang
Universal Divine Mother.

When he has attained this monistic attitude, and has
shed all sorts of dualism, when the distinction between clean
and unclean, has disappeared, the Sadhake is said ү,
have attained the stage of a Vira (hero), for he hag
conquered the pashubhava (the “animal state”) or dualism
of the common man.

“Doaitin pashin viganiyad advaito-vira uchyate”.


A very brief description of the nature and position of the

Chakras is given below, which may help the i E
{ m

readers, to some extent, to form a general idea of them ang

to indicate the points to be remembered while concentrating

The Naat Sushumnéa, which is Possessed of the qualities
Sattvas, Rajas and Tamas, is resplendant like the Sun,
Moon and Fire and whose substance is the threefold Сга,
extends from the root of the Spinal Column to the thousand-
Petalled Lotus (Sahasrara) and looks like a Dhustura flower at
the bottom. Inside the Sushumna is Vajra Nad;, inside which
is Chitring Маат, which Passes through the Chakras. The
hollow passage extending throughout the Chitrins is called
the Брадата.

The 726 Nadi, placed on its left, outside the spine, is
slightly white in colour and is of the nature of the Moon and
is the very embodiment of nectar. On the right outside of
the backbone is Prnggalz emitting poison which is of the
nature of the Sun.

These three zadis unite together at the root of the genitals
in the Maladhara and form what is called a “илет” (a
confluence of three streams ). They again come together at
the level of the eye-brows in the Ajna-chakra forming another
conflux “/rzvenz™ there, only to Separate out again. The latter is
called Mukéa-triven; ( separating conflux ) in contradistinction
from the former which is known as the VYuktatriveni ( uniting
conflux ). “Samgata dhvajamiile cha vimukta bhruviyogatah.”

shila ( spear), khatvainga (staff), khadga (sword ) and

Great Guru seated on the Sehasrara Padma ) is situated

is seated on the pericarp. Her six arms hold Vara and

Above her within a 7+z£ona is ftara-Limga, shining like a

( inner Self ) resembling a flame in its radiance. On its four
sides are sparks, surrounding a light which by its own lustre
makes visible and distinct all that exists between Mila and
Brahmarandhra. Above this again is Manas and above
Manas Chakra is the Soma Chakra (the region of Full
Moon). Within this is the Bia ‘Hamsah’. ln its lap is the
Omnipotent Parashiva with Stddha-K@li in the left, the very
Self of Eternal Bliss.

7 SAHASRARA ( Thousand-petalled Lotus). Above
Ајна Chakra is the form of Mahanada, the place where
Vayu ceases to be and the pure Intelligence ( Buddhi )
becomes manifest. Above all these, is the Parama Vyoma
(the supreme ether) wherein is Shangkhint Маат and
below the Vzsarga is the white thousand-petalled Lotus with
the petals turned downwards and its clustered filaments tinged
with the colour of the rising Sun. The fifty letters of the
alphabet ‘4’ to ‘Za’, which are also whitish in colour, go round
and round its petals twenty times. Within the Lotus is the
Full Moon shedding profuse rays, moist and cool like nectar.
Inside it, constantly shining like lightning, is the ‘Azathadi’
triangle and inside this again shines Mahashiinya (the
great void ), which is the excellent Bindu attainable by great
effort only and is worshipped in secret by Devas and Kaula
Sadhakas. It is the chief centre of liberation manifesting
the pure ‘Nirvāna Kai, subtle like a thousandth part ofa
a hair with the “4makala’—the sixteenth Kala (digit ) of the
Moon which is of red colour and shining like lightning. On


4 FOREWORD


the pericarp of this Lotus is ‘Parama Hamsal’, and above it
on an ever-blissful and white twelve-petalled lotus is seated the
Guru who is Parama Shiva himself. He is the Paramatman
and Brahma.

As the Sun dispels darkness, so does he dispel Ajnana
{ nescience ) and Moka ( delusion ) of the universe and
imparts the pure minded Yogis the knowledge by which they
realise the oneness of the /iva¢man and the Paramatman,

Below the Nirvinakald is the Fire called Nizbodhik@ which
is a form of Avyaktanada. Above it and within Nirvana
kala is Para Bindu which is both Shiva апа Shakti and in
which are all the principal deities of the universe.

The worshippers of Shiva call it the ‘abode of Shiva’, those
of Vishnu the ‘place of Parama Purusha’ or ‘Vishnu’, others
again call it the ‘place of Hari-Hara’. The devotees of the
Mahashakti call it the ‘abode of the Mahdadevi?, and other
great sages call it the ‘seat of the union of Prakriti and
Purusha.

The Amakala mentioned above is the receptacle of a
continuous flow of excellent nectar coming out of the ever-
blissful union of Mahakundaliné and Parama Shiva. Herein
is revealed the Shzvapadam (abode of Shiva ), the place of
Brahman which is free from Maya and attainable only by
Yogis by pure Jnana only. It is the seat of continuous Bliss
( nityananda ), it is here that the universe finds its rest.

8) OREWORD ग्7


The best of the Vira Szdhakas who properly worships the
Mahakundalini Shakti, who remains coiled like а serpent, is
able to achieve the highest end of human life ; and to attain
the Divine state ultimately.

The Kundalint Yoga is very much practised by the
advanced 7@ntrika Sadhakas who, as such, are called Kaulas
well-acquainted with the passage of Kundalini ( Kula-patha )
through the three Limgas which represent the three aspects
of Sthula (gross ), Sukshma ( subtle ) and Karana ( causal )
and attain the Turiya ( fourth ) stage which is beyond them
( Bhavatita) and is the excellent abode of the Guru or the
Paramashiva, This state is to be achieved by passing
through ( Bhavabhavavinirimukta ) the three subordinate
Bhavas viz., Pashu-bhiva, ( in bonds of animal propensities ),
Vira-bhava ( the stage when the Sadhaka is ready to face
any struggle to cut the bonds of Maya), the Divyabhava the
Stage in which the divinisation of the individual Self sets
in and there is an end to all forms of dualism ( Duatabhiva ).
According to the teachings of Tantra Shastras, these Bhavas
are attainable by passing through seven different Acharas or
stages of purification and modes of Sadhana ( worship ) which
have correspondences and correlations with the seven principal
Chakras in the subtle body, and the seven planes of knowledge
( Septajnanabhumi ) of Vedanta philosophy.


LAYA KRAMA IN DETAIL.

7 OREWORD Ше]


Chakra along with the sixteen vowels on the petals, are
absorbed into Vyorma-tattva and Shabdatanmatra ; and the
Vayu Bia ‘Yam’ in the body of the Kundalini will be
dissolved in the Afasha Bie ‘Han ( vapour into ether } with
Vagindriya and Shabdatanmatra which will merge in the body

The Paraskiva, Makini Shakti and other deities of the
Chakra, along with the Akdska Dija ‘Ham’ and the Shabda-
४०7८८४७८०७, will be absorbed іп Akamhara-tattva which will
merge into the body of Kundalini as she proceeds to pieree
the third or the last knot called the Rudragranthi which is
the hardest of the three.

The Kula Yogi who can pass the Kundalini and the
/iwatman through this knot is never born again in this world.
When this knot is pierced the Kundalini automatically enters
the Sažasrāra Chakra by herself. She is then coiled with the
Pavramashiva and is in complete union. Нег supreme state—-
Mahikundatinit is revealed in the form of Pranava, lustrous like
a million of suns while cool like a million moons with currents
of immortalising nectar flowing out therefrom in an
incessant stream.

The Akankara-tattva is then absorbed into Mahat-tattva or
Buddhi-tattva, Buddht-tattva into Manas-tattva, Manas-tattva
into Prakriti-tativa, Prakriti-tattva into Jwa-tattva,
Jwa-tattva into Niyati-tattva, Niyati-tattva into Kala-tattva,
Kala-tattva into Raga-tativa, Réaga-tattva into Kala-tattva,
Kalé-tativa into Avidya-tattva, Avidya-tativa into Maya-
tattva, Maya-tativa into Skhuddha-Vidya-tattva, Shuddha-
Vidya-tativa into Isvara-tativa, Tsvara-tativa into Sadashiva-
ѓайоа, Sadashiva-tativa into Shakti-tattva, Shaktt-tattva into
Shiwa-tattva, and Shiva-tativa into Parama Shiva,

“Kavalikrita-nihshesa-tattvagrima-svaripint”
—Vamikesvara Tantra

She withdraws all the thirty-six iattvas within herself

and she alone remains.


20 FOREWORD


When this last process of the absorption of the Shiva
tattva in the Parama Shiva has taken place the supreme end
of the Zaya-yog4 has been achieved. With the resorption of
the thirty-six ¢a¢évas in the Pavame-Shiva or Para-Samvit the
Samhāra or Laya-kv4ma is complete. The summum bonum
of the human life has been realised. The union of the Jīvāż-
man with the Paramatman has been effected. He has found
out his true self. The Supreme hour of his life has struck |
In a flash the veil of Māyā is torn asunder. The serene illumi-
nation of the beatific vision breaks forth in all its glory. And
the Sadhaka realises in blissful ecstasy that he is none other
than the Brahman !

“Aham brahmasmi vijnanad

Ajnano vilayo bhavet.

Sohamityeva samchiniya

Vikavet 567 vada priye”. ў
—Gandharva Tantra.

The supreme realisation that “I am nothing else but
Brahman” helps to remove all ignorance from the mind of a


devotee, who should meditate and roam about bearing in
mind the Mahavakye “Soham” —“He am I”,


Om Tat Sat.


Kalikishvam


Belur, Shri Bhaivabananda.
November, 794०0

AGAMANUSANDHANA SAMITI.


The object of the Society is the preservation and promulgation of a
knowledge of the Agama by the publication of Texts and other works on the


Shastra,
dealing with the Agamas.


LIST OF PUBLICATIONS


Vol. I, Tantribhidhana with Bijanighantu, Mudranighantu
Mantribhidhén, Miatrikiinighantu ete.—A Tantrik Dictionary. ( Revised and
enlarged 2nd Ed, )

Vol ІІ. Shatchakraniriipana. —A work on Kuzdaliniyoga with two lucid

Vidyaratna. Illustrative English introduction by Ѕутіѕгее Bhairavanaudanaths.
(зга Еа. thoroughly revised with variant readings. )

Vol. VIII. Zantraraja ( Kidimata ), Part І. Commentary by Subhaga-

English by Arthur Avalon.

Vol. IX. Karparadi-stotra. The famous hymn to Kali by Mahakala,
Text, with Introduction and Commentary containing the esoteric interpretation
of the hymn by Vimaldnanda-Svami, ‘Translated by and with notes of Arthur
Avalon,

Vol. X. Kamakalivilisa, Commentary by Na/ananandanatha. Edited
by Mahamahopadhyaya Sadashiva Mishra, Complete translation by and notes
of Arthur Avalon, This is a poetic description by Puvynanda of the unfolding
of the Divine creative impulse.


Foreword on the Vajrayana by Arthur Ayalon—


Full Summary (37 pages ) in


by the copying and suitable distribution of Texts and other works


Vol. ЖІ. Kaulopanishad, Tripurimopan ishad, Влаха nhpanishad.
Commentaries on these and the Prayoga on the Bhavana by Вһаѕкагагӣуа,
Bahvrichopanishad, Arunopanishad, with Commentary 3 Kalikopanishad, Intro-
duction by Arthur Avalon, Edited by Sitarama Shastri. The Prayoga by
Bhaskara shows how Upanisfadik teachings can and ought to be imbibed.

Vol. XII. Zantraraja. Part II. Commentary by Subhagainandanatha.
Edited by Mahamahopadhaya Sadashiva Mishra. Summary (74 pages) in
English by Arthur Avalon. This part gives the philosophy of Tantrik rituals.
It also contains a description of the four stages of uttered sound. The student
of astronomy will find much of interest in Ch, 25.

Vol. XIII. Makānirvāna Tantra, with the Commentary of Нагіһагӣ-
nanda Bharati. Printed from Mss, of Raja Rammohun Roy.

Vol. XIV. Kaulavalinirnaya. By Jnanananda Paramahamsa. A com-
pilation from various original Tantras. It gives an account of the rituals of
Kula Dharma and also contains an account of the three classes into which
mankind is divided and other important particulars necessary for an adequate
knowledge of the Sampradaya.

Vol. XV. Brakmasamhita with the commentary of Jiva Gosvamī and
Vishnusahasranama with the commentary of Shamkaracharya. Both these аге
highly venerated by Vaisknavas. The Brahma Samhita was greatly valued by
Shri-Chaitanyadeva the founder of the present day Bengal Vaisknavism, Jiva
Gosvami wrote the commentary in compliance with the desire of Chaitanyadeva.
These books show that there can be no conflict between the Vaishnava Tantras
and those of other schools.

Vols. XVI, XVII. Skāradātilaka Tantra : By Lakshmanadeshikendra.
Commentary by Raghavabhaita named Padarthadarsha—with a Summary
(72 pages) in English. This is similar in character to the Ргарапсһаѕӣга.
The text and the commentary which is very lucid are exhaustive will be of help
for a proper understanding of also the Prapanchasara the language of which is
at times rather involved. An alphabetical index of the verses is appended to
the book. Dr, Winternitz, in his History of Indian Literature, says :_«J; is Sir
John Woodroffe ( under the pseudonym of Arthur Avalon ) who by a series of
essays and the publication of the most important Tantra texts has enabled us to
form a just judgment and an objective historical idea of this religion and its
literature”. `

Vol. XX. Chidgaganchandrika—By Kalidasa, a valuable treatise on
Kashmirian Shaiva philosophy. Edited by Svami Trivikrama Tirtha,—with an
illuminating English introduction.

Vol, ХХІ, Tirabhakiisudhdrnava— By Narasimha Thakkur. Edited
by Рапсһапап Bhattacharya, Tarka-Sankhya-Vedantatirtha. It is an ex-
haustive work dealing with Dwitia Mahavidya Tara and throws a flood of


new light on the subject.
